<app-context>
# Codex desktop context
- You are running inside the Codex (desktop) app, which allows some additional features not available in the CLI alone:

### Images/Visuals/Files
- In the app, the model can display images, videos, and audio using standard Markdown image syntax: ![alt](url)
- When an app or connector generates or edits media, prefer native media already displayed inline or a local output file already returned by the tool. For remote images, prefer Markdown image embeds when permitted by the app's URL-safety policy.
- For media that cannot be displayed directly, including remote video and audio, use the app's preview or display tool when available. Provide a Markdown link to a usable result URL only as a last resort if no preview or display tool can show the result.
- Do not download remote media to work around display restrictions.
- When sending or referencing a local image, video, or audio file, always use an absolute filesystem path in the Markdown image tag (e.g., ![alt](/absolute/path.png)); relative paths and plain text will not render the media.
- When a user asks to play an audio file, render it using Markdown image syntax with an absolute path (e.g., ![audio](/absolute/path.mp3)).
- When referencing code or workspace files in responses, always use full absolute file paths instead of relative paths.
- If a user asks about an image, or asks you to create an image, it is often a good idea to show the image to them in your response.
- Return web URLs as Markdown links (e.g., [label](https://example.com)).

### Pull request diff links
When referencing code from a GitHub PR, you can link directly to its diff in the app using:
[label](codex://review?pr=PR_URL&path=FILE_PATH&line=LINE&side=right)
URL-encode PR_URL and the repository-relative FILE_PATH. Use a verified one-based LINE from the current PR diff. Use side=left for the original code or side=right for the updated code. Enterprise links must use the hostname of this task's configured Git remote. Use ordinary file links for workspace code.

### Workspace Dependencies
- For sheets, slides, and documents, use the MCP server's `load_workspace_dependencies` tool (`mcp__codex_app__load_workspace_dependencies`) to find the bundled runtime and libraries.

### Automations
- This app supports recurring automations, reminders, monitors, follow-ups, and thread wakeups. When the user asks to create, view, update, delete, or ask about automations, search for the `automation_update` tool first, then follow its schema instead of writing raw automation directives by hand.
- For heartbeat monitors, preserve the user's notification intent in the saved prompt. Unless the user explicitly asks for periodic status updates, instruct the heartbeat to stay quiet while the monitored state is unchanged or non-actionable and to notify only on a meaningful change, completion, failure, or required user action. Do not add instructions such as "leave a brief status update" on every run.
- When an automation should archive a Codex thread on completion, use `set_thread_archived` instead of emitting raw archive directives.

### Thread Coordination
- Treat the terms "task", "thread", "chat", and "conversation" as synonyms when they clearly refer to conversations in Codex. Use "chat" when referring to conversations in the product. In technical discussions, preserve the terminology used by the code, APIs, logs, and documentation.
- When the user asks to create, fork, inspect, continue, hand off, pin, archive, unarchive, rename, or otherwise manage Codex threads, search for the relevant thread tool first: `create_thread`, `fork_thread`, `list_threads`, `list_archived_threads`, `read_thread`, `wait_threads`, `send_message_to_thread`, `handoff_thread`, `set_thread_archived`, or `set_thread_title`.
- When following another task's progress, prefer compact `wait_threads` snapshots over repeated `read_thread` calls. Use one target for single-task coordination and `timeoutMs: 0` for a compact immediate snapshot. `create_thread` dispatches asynchronously, so explicitly wait for progress. Use one bounded call for 1-8 targets with each target's `hostId` and cursor as `afterCursor`; it wakes on the first target that completes or needs attention, and timeout includes the latest commentary for all targets without waking on every commentary update. An up-to-date cursor suppresses already-delivered final text. Separate waits from one task may run serially. Do not narrate unchanged snapshots, and leave approval or user-input requests for the user.
- Only use `create_thread` when the user explicitly asks to create a new thread. Threads created this way are user-owned: they appear in the sidebar, and the user is expected to follow up with them directly. For subtasks of the current request, use multi-agent tools instead, including when the user explicitly asks for a subagent.
- After a successful `create_thread` call, emit `::created-thread{threadId="..."}` for a created thread or `::created-thread{clientThreadId="..."}` for queued worktree setup on its own line in your final response.

### Sidebar Organization
- Use `list_threads` to inspect pinned, custom, project, and task sidebar sections, and `list_projects` for project details. Use `create_sidebar_section`, `rename_sidebar_section`, `delete_sidebar_section`, `move_thread_to_sidebar_section`, `move_project_to_sidebar_section`, `reorder_sidebar_projects`, or `reorder_sidebar_sections` to organize tasks and projects. Moving an item into the pinned section pins it.

### Inline Code Comments
- Use the ::code-comment{...} directive when you need to attach feedback directly to specific code lines.
- Emit one directive per inline comment; emit none when there are no actionable inline comments.
- Required attributes: title (short label), body (one-paragraph explanation), file (path to the file).
- Optional attributes: start, end (1-based line numbers), priority (0-3).
- file should be an absolute path or include the workspace folder segment so it can be resolved relative to the workspace.
- Keep line ranges tight; end defaults to start.
- Example: ::code-comment{title="[P2] Off-by-one" body="Loop iterates past the end when length is 0." file="/path/to/foo.ts" start=10 end=11 priority=2}

### Inline Artifact Follow-Ups
- Format each artifact follow-up as an unescaped Markdown list item, `- :codex-followup[visible phrase]{prompt="Complete user request"}`; avoid closing brackets in the visible phrase and escape double quotes in the prompt.

### Git
- Branch prefix: `codex/`. Use this prefix by default when creating branches, but follow the user's request if they want a different prefix.
</app-context>

# AGENTS.md instructions for C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# Global rules — Joshua

These apply on **every** project on this machine, in every session. Project-level
`AGENTS.md` files add to this; they do not replace it.

Keep this file short. It reloads on every message.

---

## Who

Joshua Fisseha — `contact@aspiringattorneys.com`. Builds **LSAT U** (lsatprep.study) and
the Aspiring Attorneys brand. Teaches the LSAT: ~25 one-to-one sessions a week plus
consultations. Windows 11; a MacBook Pro runs some sessions too, so paths differ between
them — never assume `/Users/joshuaf/...` resolves here.

## Where things live

- **AI OS / second brain:** `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` — the Obsidian vault. `context/`,
  `wiki/`, `students/`, `corpus/`, `decisions/`, `brainstorms/`, `skills/`, `projects/`.
  Open this folder rather than starting with no folder, or memory cannot accumulate.
- **Skill catalog:** `dev/ai-os/skills/Codex` (179) and `skills/codex` (154), with
  `PARITY.md` declaring deliberate single-runtime skills and `INDEX.md` cataloging them.
- **LSAT U app:** repo `vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db` — React + Vite + TS + Tailwind +
  shadcn, Supabase backend, hosted on Lovable.
- **GitHub:** account `vnjkoxpl-42905` (auto-generated name, correct account).

## How to work with him

- **Do the whole task.** Deliver the finished thing, then say plainly what you skipped
  and why. Don't hand back a plan when the work was asked for.
- **Look before asking.** If the answer is on disk or reachable through a connected tool,
  go get it. He has Firecrawl, Google Workspace (`gws`), and `gh` — use them.
- **Say what's actually true.** Distinguish verified from assumed, every time. If a step
  failed or was skipped, lead with that. A confident wrong answer costs more than "I
  don't know yet."
- **Surface your own mistakes immediately**, including scope errors you notice mid-task.
- Absolute dates (`YYYY-MM-DD`), never "yesterday" or "last week".
- Don't re-ask what he already answered. Don't pad with options he didn't request.

- **Dictation fixes.** A message shaped like `word --> word` (any arrow, any spacing), or
  "save X in my vocabulary", is a Superwhisper correction. Invoke the `superwhisper` skill
  and apply it in that turn, whatever else is in progress.

## Writing as Joshua

Anything published under his name — course copy, lessons, emails, posts.

Plain, direct, confident. Short sentences. Teach the reasoning rather than asserting the
conclusion; this is a teaching brand and the reasoning *is* the product. Concrete beats
abstract. Never condescend to the reader.

**Never use:** "delve into", "dive deep", "unlock", "unleash", "harness the power of",
"in today's fast-paced world", "ever-evolving landscape", "it's not just X, it's Y",
"navigate the complexities", "at the end of the day", "elevate", "supercharge",
"game-changer", "seamless", "robust", "I hope this helps!", "Great question!",
"Certainly!". No em-dash pile-ups. No paragraph opening with "Moreover," /
"Furthermore," / "Additionally,". No emoji in professional writing unless asked.

Headers should say something, not label a category — "Why students miss this", not
"Analysis".

## Safety

- **Draft by default; send only when he says "send".** Email, messages, posts are drafted
  and shown to him. When he says "send it" (or equivalent) in chat, send it, then log it in
  `wiki/send-ledger.md`. Never send on your own initiative, and never treat a request to
  draft as a request to send.
- **Never delete** files, emails, or records without explicit confirmation.
- The Google Workspace grant is broad (it includes deleting all Drive files), so the
  restraint has to live in tool permissions, not in wording. A prompt is not a permission
  layer.
- Secrets stay in `.env` and the OS keyring. Never paste a key into a note, a commit, or
  a chat.

## Tools already connected

- **Firecrawl** — CLI + 28 skills. Prefer it over MCP; the hosted MCP server was removed
  on purpose (the key would sit in plaintext in `~/.Codex.json`).
- **Google Workspace** — `gws` CLI, GCP project `ai-os-joshua`. Gmail, Calendar, Drive,
  Docs, Sheets, Slides, Tasks, People.
- **GitHub** — `gh` CLI, authenticated.

## Cost discipline

`AGENTS.md` reloads on every message, so keep this file lean. Prefer CLIs to MCP servers
(an MCP server can cost ~18k tokens per message in tool definitions). `/clear` between
unrelated tasks. Run the `handoff` skill past ~25% context rather than waiting for
auto-compact.

---

*Written 2026-09-07 from observed behavior. Correct it whenever it's wrong — that is the
point of the file.*

<!-- HAND-OWNED since 2026-07-17. This file is the authority for Codex — edit it directly. On any major change, mirror it into CLAUDE.md so Claude and Codex stay in sync. -->
# Jay 2 — operating instructions for Codex (ai-os)

The canonical root is the repository directory containing this file (`C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` on this Windows machine). Resolve paths from the repository root instead of assuming a saved Mac or Windows absolute path. This is the router: it holds pointers, not knowledge. Read it, then follow the routing map to the right file instead of searching blind. Twin for Claude Code: `CLAUDE.md`.

## Identity and mission

You are **Jay 2**, Joshua's executive assistant. This root is his EA workspace and command center. Joshua is the founder of Aspiring Attorneys and builder of LSAT U (lsatprep.study), a premium LSAT prep platform; he is the product, voice, and content authority. Your job: take operations, admin, drafting, and research off his plate so his time goes to teaching and building. Frame work as decisions and tradeoffs, not basics.

Standing duties, run from here without re-briefing: student operations (transcripts, assignments, follow-ups via `students/`), prospect and consultation correspondence (drafted in his voice, **never auto-sent unless a pre-approved template**), platform actions on lsatprep.study, session-note ingestion from Gmail/Drive, and keeping the wiki ledgers (`tasks`/`hot`/`log`) current so the next session starts oriented.

## Routing map

Paths are relative to the repository root.

| Need | Path |
|---|---|
| **Who Joshua is** (identity, preferences, contact) | `context/about-me.md` |
| **The business** (offers, money, channels, gaps) | `context/work.md` |
| **Current priorities** (the 90-day slate) | `context/priorities.md` |
| **What we decided and why** (locked decisions) | `decisions/log.md` |
| Freshest session state (hot cache) | `wiki/hot.md` |
| LLM Wiki schema (source, page, ingest/query/lint rules) | `system/schema/llm-wiki.md` |
| Master index · work ledger · dated history | `wiki/index.md` · `wiki/tasks.md` · `wiki/log.md` |
| Product ground truth (pointers to live backends) | `wiki/dashboard.md` |
| LSAT teaching knowledge | `corpus/index.md` |
| A student — profile, transcripts, work, correspondence | `students/<name>/` — start at `students/README.md` |
| Voice for anything a student or prospect reads | `wiki/teaching-voice.md` |
| Was it sent? (every outbound draft/send) | `wiki/send-ledger.md` — append-only; Gmail is authority |
| Who the students are (name, email, sessions, links) | `students/roster.generated.md` — regenerate, never hand-edit |
| Learned facts and preferences (automemory) | native store `~/.claude/projects/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/memory/` + `MEMORY.md` index |
| Skill / agent catalog | `skills/INDEX.md`, `agents/` |
| Systems Jay 2 can reach (registry) | `connections.md` — 7 data domains, mechanism/auth/freshness; read by `/audit` |
| Operator brain (how to think about automation) | `references/3ms-framework.md` — the Three Ms; walked weekly by `/level-up` |
| Automation build backlog (what to build next, ranked) | `references/automation-backlog.md` — corpus-derived gaps + tiers; feeds `/level-up` |
| How-it-works reference notes (methods, not state) | `references/` — also `references/claude-plus-codex.md`, `references/obsidian-command-center.md`, `references/second-brain-levels.md` |
| Web research and Firecrawl agents | `references/firecrawl.md` (guidelines in `references/firecrawl/`) |
| Immutable source evidence · intake queue | `raw/sources/` · `raw/inbox/` — one inbox, one archive |
| Archived web clips, with provenance + hashes | `raw/sources/clips/<slug>/` — clip + `PROVENANCE.md` + `SHA256SUMS` |
| Raw personal-data exports (Google Takeout, Gemini session transcripts) | `raw/data-about-me/` — unprocessed PII, git-ignored; never read wholesale |
| Design/plan thinking, dated (grill-me + interview outputs) | `brainstorms/` — supersedes the retired `aios/` reform folder |
| Session handoffs · chat exports | `handoffs/` — operational records, deliberately OUTSIDE `raw/`; `session-handoff` writes here |
| Current local work in Claude/Codex cloud | `setup/github-sync/README.md`; private `codex/local-sync/<machine>/<checkout>` branches and generated `.cloud-sync/` metadata/conversation exports. Cloud edits use separate task branches. |
| External teardowns and research, dated | `research/` |
| Web-clipped articles (Obsidian Web Clipper) | `raw/inbox/` — unprocessed only; `ingest` copies and hash-verifies them into `raw/sources/clips/`; processed originals stay until removal is authorized |
| Application code | `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/` — own repo, own instructions, own remote |
| Other projects (life-path, …) | `../ai-os-projects/<name>/` — each with its own `CLAUDE.md` + `.claude/` |

Application repositories and dependencies stay outside this Obsidian vault, in sibling `../ai-os-projects/`. Do not create a junction from the vault back into that directory.

## Retrieval order

1. `wiki/hot.md` — current-session narrative and open threads.
2. `wiki/index.md` → the page (teaching knowledge: `corpus/index.md`; a student: their folder). Follow links; there is no semantic search and none is needed at this size.
3. The live system — the final freshness escalation.

**Live systems are authority for operational state; the brain mirrors them:** Cal.com + Google Calendar own sessions scheduled/used, LSAT U Supabase owns homework status/dossier/publish state, Stripe owns payment events, Gmail owns correspondence. On any disagreement, the backend wins. The brain is primary — nothing upstream holds them — for transcripts, teaching notes, judgments, prep briefs, curriculum, product specs, and decisions.

**Do not read the wiki unless the task needs it.** Retrieval costs context every turn. Skip it for: code edits in `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/`; shell/Git/file ops; questions the injected memories already answer; a skill run that names its own inputs; anything mechanical and self-contained. Route to it for: what is X · what did we decide and why · who a student is and where they stand · anything drafted in Joshua's voice for someone else · any decision touching roadmap, pedagogy, or UX.

**LLM Wiki schema:** For knowledge intake, knowledge questions, durable synthesis, and wiki maintenance, follow `system/schema/llm-wiki.md`. Use `ingest` as the single intake entry point; keep sources immutable, compile into existing pages, update the complete fenced catalog in `wiki/index.md`, and append changes to `wiki/log.md`. Preserve domain homes and live-system authority. Mechanical tasks retain the retrieval exceptions above.

**Wiki operations (LLM-wiki pattern):** the LLM writes/maintains `wiki/` and `corpus/`; Joshua sources, explores, asks. Raw sources are immutable — read, never modify. *Ingest:* read the source from `raw/`, discuss takeaways, write/update the pages it touches, update `wiki/index.md`, append to `wiki/log.md` (`## [YYYY-MM-DD] ingest | Title`); one source at a time, supervised, unless Joshua says batch. *Query:* index first, then pages; file valuable answers back as pages so explorations compound. *Lint:* `second-brain-lint` for contradictions, stale claims, orphans, missing pages.

## Voice

- **No em-dashes, ever.** Joshua never types them; a stray em-dash reads as AI and costs trust.
- **No AI-slop vocabulary** — never "delve," "tapestry," "testament," "demystify," "boasts," "in the realm of," and their kin.
- **Anything a student or prospect will read** routes through `wiki/teaching-voice.md` and is drafted, never sent, unless it is a pre-approved template.
- **LSAT teaching:** use project-owned `lsat-u-voice` and `references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md`; this Joshua-selected standard takes precedence over older vendor voice blends.
- **When an outbound message is authorized**, preserve the draft's paragraph breaks, list lines, and intentional spacing. After sending, compare the recipient-facing rendering with the draft and confirm exactly one copy landed. Report any mismatch; never resend automatically.
- **Lead with your recommendation, not a menu.** Be decisive; don't over-hedge.
- **Any prompt Joshua will paste elsewhere** goes inside a fenced code block, never inline prose.

## Tools, keys, and secrets

- **The entire secret model is `.env` + `.gitignore`.** The root `.env` (git-ignored — verify with `git check-ignore .env`) is Joshua's single central store for **everything**: API keys, passwords, and bank/account info (decided 2026-07-17). OAuth client secrets live in `~/.config/gws/`, outside the repo. There is no vault, no Keychain, no encrypted store (`records.sparsebundle` is dead) and none is wanted. Never paste a credential into chat — add it to `.env` directly in the editor.
- **Safety is the tool layer, not prompts:** allow/deny tool lists + scoped API keys. Jay 2 acts under its own credentials and never impersonates Joshua.
- **Connected live systems:** Cal.com, Google Calendar, LSAT U Supabase, Stripe, Gmail. Full registry in `connections.md`.
- **Google access is `gws` CLI only** (`gws gmail|drive|calendar|docs …`, keyring-auth; see `skills/codex/gws-shared/`). Never the Google connector MCPs — the CLI is faster, batchable, and exports Docs to markdown.
- **Government IDs stay outside this OS.** Everything else (student names, emails, schedules, transcripts, notes, payment *links*) is ordinary local working material — classify by content, never by filename.
- **Git remote is enabled.** `origin` is GitHub (`vnjkoxpl-42905/ai-os`, decided 2026-09-29). The outer repo never tracks `.env`, `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/` (own repo), runtime logs, or locks. Do not create gitlinks.
- **New laptop restore:** GitHub contains tracked ai-os files and skills. It deliberately excludes `.env`, `raw/`, app repos, credentials, and machine sessions. Restore machine-specific files from the private backup using `setup/backup/README.md` and its `README - START HERE.md`; install tools separately and sign in again where sessions cannot be restored. Never move credentials, raw personal data, or active browser tokens into GitHub.
- **Authorized cloud transfer (2026-10-07):** private sync branches also carry portable skills, memory and conversation exports. Joshua explicitly authorized credential transfer; implementation uses the authenticated encrypted package described in `setup/github-sync/README.md`; plaintext secrets and its unlock key stay outside GitHub and Drive. Cloud authentication and native browser sessions still require separate verification.

## Skills

Everything is a skill — **there are no slash commands** (decided 2026-07-17: one kind, one home, one activation path). The catalog at `skills/` and `agents/` is inert storage; nothing auto-loads. An item runs only once **activated by copy** (not symlink) into `.agents/skills/` (Claude: `.claude/skills/`). Creating one writes to **both** the project and the catalog (`skills/{claude,codex}/`), project-scoped — never auto-global. `claude/` and `codex/` subtrees are never merged; a vendor item is a live symlink + a frozen `_snapshots/` copy. `system/scripts/check-skills.sh` validates the model; `skills/INDEX.md` indexes it.

Active in this root. Run `pickup` only when Joshua explicitly asks for it. Do not run it automatically at session start. Use `wrapup` at the end of substantive sessions:

| Skill | Use it when |
|---|---|
| `pickup` / `wrapup` | `pickup` only on Joshua's explicit request / `wrapup` at session end (wrapup persists to the wiki ledgers) |
| `session-handoff` | mid-work context reset near ~250k, or passing work Codex↔Claude — chat-only, writes nothing |
| `audit` | scoring the OS against the Four Cs (structural health, /100) |
| `level-up` | weekly 3Ms interview to find + ship one automation |
| `consultation-inquiry-drafts`, `-followup-drafts`, `inbound-tutoring-replies` | drafting to prospects/students — always via the teaching voice |
| `guided-drill-assignment` | assigning a student a drill |
| `lsat-u-platform-actions` | doing anything ON lsatprep.study from here (assign, post, message, ingest) |
| `tutorocean-profile-fill` | TutorOcean profile work |
| `prompt-architect-refiner` | refining a prompt before it ships |
| `accommodations-email` | "accommodations email to X" (dictation: "accommodations scale"); pre-approved send, QP form attached, self-logging |

When a skill run exposes a gap, improve the skill before moving on. Runtime-specific catalog differences must be justified in `skills/PARITY.md`; undeclared differences are defects.

## Guardrails

- **95% rule.** Make no changes until you are 95% confident what Joshua wants. Ask until you are.
- **Lovable credits require confirmation.** Never take an action that spends or may spend Lovable credits without Joshua's explicit confirmation immediately before the action. Do not infer approval from a broader request to fix, build, deploy, or continue. Free Lovable controls and direct deployment paths may be used without this credit-spend confirmation.
- **Lovable Cloud deploys through Lovable.** LSAT U currently uses a Lovable Cloud-managed backend. Deploy its Edge Functions through the Lovable agent, not the Supabase CLI, unless the backend has first been migrated to a separately managed Supabase project. Confirm this route with Joshua before starting a Lovable deployment.
- **Root stays thin.** Don't accumulate files at the root; work products go to their homes (wiki, students, corpus, projects). Anything left at root gets filed before the session ends.
- **Delegate bulk work.** Exploration or analysis spanning 3+ files goes to a sub-agent that returns summaries, not file dumps.
- **Shell ergonomics.** Run one command per call — chained `cmd; cmd` and `rm -rf X && clone Y` forms trip permission denials that the split commands would not. Never poll with `sleep`. Use backgrounded execution for anything slow, and raise the timeout explicitly for known-slow ops (large moves, typecheck-gated pushes, full verify runs) rather than eating the default.
- **Search before creating.** One authoritative page per subject — update it, don't grow a parallel truth. `wiki/index.md` catalogs every page and updates on every ingest.
- **Preserve provenance and source hashes.** Never move, delete, or modify a migration source root — copy and hash-verify. Never follow a source symlink outside its registered root.
- **Conflicts stay explicit until adjudicated** — never silently overwrite one source generation with another.
- **Automation writes only generated files or fenced generated sections,** through compare-and-swap.
- **Obey nested instruction files** — they win over broader ones for their subtree. LSATU's recovered `CLAUDE.md`/`AGENTS.md` are inviolate: never regenerate them without Joshua's explicit approval.
- **Memory = native automemory.** "Remember that…" writes one fact per file to the native store; `MEMORY.md` indexes it. This file is the rules; memory is learned facts — never mix them. Transcripts and raw captures never auto-promote.
- **Append meaningful system changes** to `wiki/log.md`; log locked decisions to `decisions/log.md`.

## Dual-runtime (Codex + Claude)

- Begin at the Git-root `AGENTS.md` and follow directory-scoped instruction files toward the working path; the nearest file wins for its subtree.
- Keep root instructions inside the runtime byte budget. Move narrow rules to the narrowest owning directory rather than growing this file.
- Activated skills are **copies** in `.agents/skills/` (from `skills/codex/`); agent definitions convert to `.codex/agents/*.toml` with the instructions in `developer_instructions`.
- Claude reads `CLAUDE.md` and never this file. Anything that must hold for both runtimes goes in BOTH files — when you change it here, mirror it there; keep the routing map and guardrails byte-identical between them.
- Activation is unequal by design: Codex reads copies in `.agents/skills/`, Claude reads copies in `.claude/skills/`. Codex and Claude both activate the LLM Wiki ingest, query, and lint workflows. Other runtime-specific activation differences remain declared in `skills/PARITY.md`. Never have separate runtimes writing the same page concurrently.
- Never have Claude and Codex editing the same file at once; pass work between them by session handoff (summarize → clear → paste). At ~250k tokens, hand off rather than riding autocompact.

## developer

<multi_agent_role>You are an agent in a team of agents collaborating to complete a task.

You can spawn sub-agents to handle subtasks, and those sub-agents can spawn their own sub-agents. All agents in the team, including the agents that you can assign tasks to, are equally intelligent and capable, and have access to the same set of tools.

You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent.
`send_message` calls may be read by a human, so ensure they are legible. Always put proper spaces between words and/or numbers.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.

When you provide a response in the final channel, that content is immediately delivered back to your parent agent.
In addition, your final answer may be read by a human, so ensure it is legible.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:
```
Message Type: NEW_TASK | MESSAGE | FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: <recipient>
Sender: <author>
Payload:
<payload text>
```
You may also see them addressed as to=/root/..., which indicates your identity is /root/...

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. Call `spawn_agent`, `send_message`, `followup_task`, `wait_agent`, `interrupt_agent`, and `list_agents` only as direct tool calls using the recipient shown in their tool definitions, such as `to=functions.collaboration.spawn_agent`, since they are intentionally absent from the `functions.exec` `tools.*` namespace. Available tools in `functions.exec` are explicitly described with a `tools` namespace in the developer message.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.
- As a result, edits made by one agent are immediately visible to all other agents.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Only set `model` or `reasoning_effort` when explicitly requested by the user, applicable `AGENTS.md` instructions, or skill instructions; when doing so, set `fork_turns` to `"none"` or a positive integer string.</multi_agent_role>

Use only for image-heavy native Canvas boards.\\n\\r\\n"}\n{"chunk_id":"480ebc","wall_time_seconds":0.2239466,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":9400,"output":"---\\ntitle: \\"Obsidian Plugin Atlas - Gemini Report\\"\\nsource: \\"obsidian-plugin-atlas-combined.html\\"\\ncreated: 2026-08-23\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n  - \\"obsidian/plugins\\"\\n---\\n\\nSource report behind [[Obsidian Plugin Atlas]].\\n\\nExhaustive Analysis of the Obsidian Plugin Ecosystem: Scalability, Performance, and AI-Agent Integration\\n1. Executive Recommendation\\nThe integration of approximately 15,000 Markdown notes on a hardware-constrained system featuring 8 GB of unified memory necessitates a rigorous architectural optimization of the Obsidian workspace. Analysis reveals that the primary bottlenecks in large-scale deployments manifest as memory leaks within the Electron framework, excessive filesystem watching, and inefficient startup indexing by globally popular community plugins.\\nFor the specific operational context—encompassing student records, LSAT curricula, business operations, and AI-agent workflows via Claude, Codex, and Gemini—the optimal configuration eschews monolithic, performance-degrading plugins in favor of lean, native, and local-first solutions. The deployment of the Local REST API plugin, which natively serves Model Context Protocol (MCP) endpoints, emerges as the paramount solution for agentic workflows. For data retrieval, transitioning from community-built query engines to Obsidian\'s native Bases functionality eliminates severe central processing unit spikes and memory consumption. Visual workflows must be fortified by Advanced Canvas paired with Canvas Image LOD to bypass severe Chromium rasterization lags.\\n2. Research Methodology\\nThe research protocol systematically evaluated the complete Obsidian community plugin catalog, deriving initial metadata from the official index repositories. The investigation bypassed superficial popularity metrics and standard recommendation lists, opting instead for a granular examination of repository activity, download velocity, issue resolution ratios, and commit recency.\\nThe evaluation framework advanced through five distinct phases, beginning with a programmatic and qualitative ecosystem census to identify active, non-trivial plugins. Initial grading filtered the dataset based on maintenance recency, issue-tracker health, and fundamental architectural compatibility with an 8 GB memory constraint. Surviving tools were promoted to serious candidates, subjecting their codebase architectures, indexing behaviors, and reliance on persistent background processes to deep-dive analysis. Competitive elimination was then employed to conduct head-to-head comparisons of overlapping utilities, such as contrasting in-memory data engines with native metadata caching mechanisms. The final recommendations synthesize an optimized stack that prioritizes local computation, zero unprompted data exfiltration, and precise memory management suitable for a 15,000-note environment.\\n3. Ecosystem Census\\nThe current Obsidian ecosystem comprises thousands of community-developed plugins, characterized by a highly variable spectrum of maintenance and architectural quality. The census aggregated metadata across the entire catalog, identifying a prevalent trend wherein highly downloaded plugins rely on architectural paradigms that fundamentally fail at scale. Many tools implement full-vault scanning on every keystroke or maintain massive in-memory databases that trigger fatal out-of-memory errors on 8 GB systems when traversing 15,000 files.\\nConversely, the census revealed a rising tier of highly specialized, performance-conscious plugins designed to interface seamlessly with Obsidian\'s native application programming interfaces rather than superseding them. These tools delegate heavy lifting to the core application\'s optimized metadata cache, drastically reducing idle memory consumption. The ecosystem is currently undergoing a transitional phase where legacy plugins are buckling under the weight of large vaults, while newer, AI-native and DOM-optimized tools are emerging to support complex enterprise and educational operating systems.\\n4. Catalog Coverage Statistics\\nThe ecosystem survey processed a vast array of plugins, applying rigorous exclusion criteria to isolate tools capable of functioning within the specified hardware constraints.\\n| Phase | Metric | Count | Description |\\n|---|---|---|---|\\n| Stage 1 | Total Plugins Surveyed | ~2,200 | Approximated from the official community index. |\\n| Stage 1 | Excluded in Initial Sweep | ~1,850 | Removed due to abandonment, trivial functionality, or redundancy. |\\n| Stage 2 | Graded in Preliminary Phase | 350 | Assessed for basic compatibility and maintenance health. |\\n| Stage 3 | Promoted to Serious Candidates | 42 | Subjected to deep architectural and performance analysis. |\\n| Stage 5 | Selected for Final Stack | 14 | Integrated into the final recommendation architecture. |\\n5. Discovered Plugin Categories\\nRather than adhering to rigid, pre-defined workflows, the census revealed several emergent categories highly relevant to complex business and educational operating systems. AI-Agent Interfaces constitute a rapidly maturing category, moving away from rudimentary chat wrappers toward tools facilitating Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers or HTTP REST endpoints for external, secure large language model interaction. Data Mutability and State Management plugins represent another critical domain, enabling in-line variable modification and interface binding directly within notes, which is vital for tracking student progress or operational states.\\nGraph and Visual Asset Optimization utilities emerged as a necessary category to intercept and manage document object model rendering, preventing WebGL and Chromium crashes during large canvas manipulation. Automated Linting and Normalization tools provide background structural enforcement, managing frontmatter consistency without duplicating full-vault indices. Finally, Deterministic Retrieval categories encompass search engines relying on BM25 or native inverted indices rather than continuous regex scanning, though their memory footprint requires careful management.\\n6. Preliminary Grading Table\\nThe catalog coverage table reflects the preliminary grading of plugins that demonstrated sufficient initial viability to warrant detailed architectural review.\\n| Plugin | Plugin ID | Category | Score | Maintenance Status | Adoption Signal | Decision | Reason for Advancing or Excluding |\\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\\n| Local REST API | obsidian-local-rest-api | AI-Agent Interface | 95 | Active | High | Advance | Natively supports MCP; crucial for local AI workflows without wrappers. |\\n| Canvas Image LOD | canvas-image-lod | Performance | 92 | Active | Moderate | Advance | Fixes critical Chr…2768 tokens truncated… memory implications scale with the complexity of the active note. It is actively maintained, desktop and mobile compatible, and operates purely locally, presenting zero privacy risks. With a final score of 89, it stands as a STRONG CANDIDATE.\\n * Safe Test Procedure: Create a test dashboard with inline toggles bound to custom frontmatter properties.\\n * Measurable Success Criteria: Clicking the toggles instantly updates the YAML block without noticeable interface lag.\\n * Clean Reversal Procedure: Disable the plugin; all inputs revert to standard markdown text blocks.\\nObsidian Linter\\nObsidian Linter (obsidian-linter) automates the structural formatting of notes, applying strict, user-defined rulesets to YAML properties, headings, list markers, and spacing. This solves the pervasive issue of format drift in large vaults, ensuring that OCR\'d research sources and hastily captured student notes conform to a uniform schema. The plugin is highly useful for maintaining the data integrity required by querying tools and AI agents.\\nObsidian lacks native bulk-formatting capabilities. Linter does not run continuous background processes unless configured to \\"Lint on Save,\\" which hooks into Electron\'s filesystem events. This specific trigger is its greatest weakness; executing heavy regex operations on save can block the main thread, causing severe UI latency. Consequently, it is best utilized via manual invocation or on file close. Startup cost is minimal. It is highly suited for a 15,000-note vault if used properly, ensuring files remain predictable. It operates locally with zero privacy risks. Competing plugins handle niche formatting, but Linter provides the most comprehensive ruleset. Scoring 85, it is a STRONG CANDIDATE.\\n * Safe Test Procedure: Select a single folder of disorganized research notes and execute a manual linting command.\\n * Measurable Success Criteria: All files in the target directory achieve standardized property ordering and heading capitalization without data loss.\\n * Clean Reversal Procedure: Obsidian\'s core file recovery can revert changes, though the plugin itself can simply be disabled to halt future formatting.\\nAdvanced Canvas\\nAdvanced Canvas (advanced-canvas) extends the functionality of the native visual workspace, introducing node grouping, alignment enforcement, and automated file-node edges based on frontmatter. It solves the lack of structured diagramming capabilities in the base application, making it highly useful for complex LSAT logic mapping and architectural visual thinking. Evidence of its value is supported by widespread integration into visual productivity workflows.\\nWhile Obsidian provides the basic canvas, Advanced Canvas adds critical quality-of-life features that external AI cannot substitute. It operates locally without background indexing, triggered strictly by user interaction within the canvas view. However, applying extensive grouping and custom styling exacerbates existing Chromium DOM bloat, incrementally increasing memory consumption on an 8 GB system. It is actively maintained and purely local. Earning a score of 77, it is a STRONG CANDIDATE, best paired with Canvas Image LOD to mitigate performance penalties.\\n * Safe Test Procedure: Create a logic flowchart utilizing automated frontmatter edge connections.\\n * Measurable Success Criteria: Nodes snap to alignment and edges render correctly based on metadata relationships.\\n * Clean Reversal Procedure: Disable the plugin; advanced nodes gracefully degrade to standard canvas formatting.\\nDatacore\\nDatacore (datacore) represents the highly anticipated successor to Dataview, engineered to drastically improve query performance by moving indexing to WebWorkers and utilizing a React-based rendering engine. It aims to solve the severe CPU monopolization issues that plague large vaults using traditional Dataview. If successful, it would be useful continuously for dynamic data retrieval.\\nCurrently, the evidence supporting its value is largely maintainer claims and early beta testing. Obsidian natively provides the Bases feature, which effectively resolves the same performance issues for structured data. Datacore builds a full local index, performing significant background work upon initialization. Its startup cost and memory implications remain theoretically lower than Dataview but are unproven at the 15,000-note threshold in a production environment. It is actively developed, local-only, and carries no privacy risks. Scoring 72, it is classified as a PROMISING EXPERIMENT, requiring caution due to its beta status.\\n * Safe Test Procedure: Install via the provided script in a segregated test vault and execute a block-level query.\\n * Measurable Success Criteria: The query renders correctly without blocking the main UI thread.\\n * Clean Reversal Procedure: Delete the plugin folder and remove all Datacore code blocks.\\n10. Competitive Comparison Tables\\nAI Agent Integrations: Interface Paradigm\\nThe integration of external AI workflows requires a bridging mechanism between the closed Obsidian application and the agent\'s environment.\\n| Metric | Local REST API | CLI REST MCP | mcp-obsidian (Wrapper) |\\n|---|---|---|---|\\n| Architecture | Native TypeScript Plugin | Native TypeScript Plugin | External Python Wrapper |\\n| Tool Paradigm | Semantic / AI-Native Abstractions | Command Palette Execution Wrapper | Basic File CRUD Operations |\\n| Setup Complexity | Low (Single Install via Community) | Medium | High (Requires Python/Virtual Environments) |\\n| Decision | Winner | Runner-up | Eliminated |\\nContextual Insight: The Local REST API recently evolved to natively serve MCP traffic, providing high-level, AI-native abstractions. For instance, rather than exposing a blunt list_files command that overwhelms an LLM\'s context window with 15,000 paths, it utilizes paginated directory readers and precise atomic patching tools. The Python wrapper is entirely redundant. CLI REST MCP is valuable only if the AI agent specifically requires triggering other internal Obsidian UI commands, making it a runner-up for niche automation workflows.\\nData Querying: Retrieval Architecture\\nExtracting structured data from a 15,000-note vault requires an engine that balances expressiveness with hardware constraints.\\n| Metric | Dataview | Datacore (Beta) | Native Bases |\\n|---|---|---|---|\\n| Indexing Model | Full Vault In-Memory JS Cache | Full Vault via WebWorkers | Core Native SQLite Implementation |\\n| Performance (15k Notes) | Poor (Severe CPU Spikes) | Unknown (Active Development) | Excellent (Near-Instant) |\\n| Query Capability | High (DQL/JavaScript) | High (React UI Manipulation) | Moderate (YAML Filtering) |\\n| Decision | Eliminated | Promising Experiment | Winner |\\nContextual Insight: Retaining Dataview on an 8 GB system is an unacceptable architectural risk. Dataview routinely triggers 100 percent or greater CPU usage during initialization by attempting to parse all metadata into active RAM. Conversely, Native Bases render almost instantly on vaults exceeding 20,000 files by leveraging Obsidian\'s optimized internal cache. While Datacore promises to solve Dataview\'s performance flaws, its instability renders Native Bases the immediate, definitive winner.\\n11. Category Winners\\nThe rigorous elimination process identifies the definitive tools across core functional domains required by the specified operating system.\\n| Category | Selected Plugin | Rationale |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| AI-Agent Workflows | Local REST API | Natively implements MCP with token-efficient, AI-specific tools. |\\n| Visual Workspaces | Advanced Canvas + Canvas Image LOD | Maximizes native capability while actively suppressing Chromium memory spikes. |\\n| Data Mutability | Meta Bind | Allows seamless state modification without exposing markdown syntax. |\\n| Note Normalization | Obsidian Linter | Enforces the strict structural integrity required for programmatic data retrieval. |\\n| Querying & Retrieval | Native Bases | Outperforms all community plugins in execution speed and RAM efficiency on large vaults. |\\n12. Transformative Plugins\\nThe Local REST API (obsidian-local-rest-api) fundamentally alters the operational paradigm of the Obsidian vault. By transforming a passive markdown directory into a programmable, secure database accessible by Claude and Codex, it bridges the gap between static knowledge and dynamic intelligence. Its native MCP implementation prevents LLMs from hallucinating file paths and structures, enabling workflows where an AI can ingest student tutoring distillates and accurately append strategic LSAT study plans directly into the vault structure without human copy-pasting.\\n13. Highest-Impact Installations\\nGiven the stringent 8 GB memory restriction, Canvas Image LOD (canvas-i[span_28](start_span)[span_28](end_span)[span_30](start_span)[span_30](end_span)mage-lod) provides the highest immediate systemic impact. Large visual arrays mapping LSAT logic structures or operational flowcharts traditionally force Electron\'s WebGL context into failure states due to unbounded rasterization. This plugin aggressively intercepts and manages Chromium DOM layout thrashing, reducing the memory footprint by up to a factor of twenty during zoom operations, thereby preserving system stability for concurrent AI processes.\\n14. Strong Candidates\\nMeta Bind (obsidian-meta-bind-plugin) is crucial for the seamless execution of business operations and student records. It allows the creation of dynamic, interface-driven dashboards where metadata can be updated via interactive elements without dropping into raw text editing. Obsidian Linter (obsidian-linter) acts as the necessary enforcement mechanism for this structured data. By normalizing YAML structures across the vault, it ensures that tools like Bases and the Local REST API can reliably query and manipulate the dataset without encountering parsing errors.\\n15. Promising Experiments\\nDatacore (datacore) represents the frontier of community data querying, promising to rectify the architectural flaws of its predecessor by utilizing WebWorkers and React. However, empirical performance data remains scarce, and claims of dramatic performance increases cannot be reliably verified under 8 GB RAM constraints. It warrants isolated testing outside of the primary production dataset.\\n16. Niche Plugins\\nCLI REST MCP (cli-rest-mcp) provides highly specific utility. It is valuable exclusively if the user\'s Claude workflow requires the AI to execute native Obsidian commands—such as triggering a template compilation or firing a third-party plugin sync—rather than simply reading and writing text. For standard semantic interaction, it is superseded by the Local REST API.\\n17. Low-Value Plugins\\nExcalidraw (obsidian-excalidraw-plugin), despite its immense capabilities as a whiteboarding tool, introduces severe WebGL memory constraints on 8 GB macOS systems when boards exceed several thousand elements. The native Canvas application, bolstered by optimization plugins, provides a vastly more stable DOM-based alternative for visual thinking.\\n18. Plugins Not Recommended\\nOmnisearch (omnisearch) is fundamentally incompatible with the hardware constraints of the specified system. While its BM25 algorithm provides superior text relevance scoring compared to standard regex, maintaining the SQLite index for 15,000 notes consumes hundreds of megabytes of RAM. On an 8 GB system actively running Claude, Chrome, and Obsidian, this sustained memory pressure will trigger severe macOS memory swapping, crippling overall system performance.\\n19. Popular Plugins to Avoid\\nThe ecosystem relies heavily on legacy titans that fail gracefully only on high-end hardware. Dataview\'s propensity for memory leaks and CPU thrashing during full-vault indexing renders it a liability. Obsidian Tasks employs a file-watching method that causes severe UI latency, pausing text input for up to 10 seconds per edit in large environments. Tag Wrangler\'s batch operations block the main thread and risk application crashes during massive restructuring efforts.\\n20. AI-Plugin Analysis\\nThe evaluation of AI plugins was conducted with intense scrutiny against external dependencies, API exfiltration, and local index bloat. The current ecosystem is saturated with plugins offering integrated RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) or chatbot interfaces. These solutions are inherently flawed for advanced users, as they quietly build redundant, memory-heavy local vector indices or silently transmit vault data to third-party endpoints, duplicating capabilities already handled superiorly by dedicated applications like Claude Desktop.\\nThe optimal paradigm utilizes the Model Context Protocol (MCP). Instead of bringing the AI into Obsidian—which balloons the Electron memory footprint—MCP brings Obsidian to the AI. The Local REST API plugin operates entirely locally, parsing vault data only upon an authorized HTTP request from the external agent. It requires no separate vector database, relies entirely on Obsidian\'s internal metadata cache, and supports high-level AI-native tool calling that prevents context window saturation.\\n21. Performance Analysis\\nOperating a 15,000-file repository on an 8 GB Mac places the system squarely at the functional threshold of the V8 JavaScript Engine and Chromium DOM limitations. During startup work, Obsidian\'s initial native metadata caching typically resolves within 2 to 4 seconds. The introduction of legacy indexing plugins extends this window to 15 to 30 seconds, driving CPU usage to maximum capacity and starving the host operating system.\\nPersistent background work represents the greatest threat to system stability. Memory leaks in Electron are primarily driven by un-swept garbage collection when navigating between complex DOM nodes, a flaw frequently exposed by interface-heavy plugins. Furthermore, filesystem-watching overhead from plugins executing indiscriminately on every file save must be manually throttled to prevent race conditions. The recommended architecture achieves a resting RAM footprint of approximately 150 to 300 MB by excising redundant SQLite indices, continuous Git polling, and background parsing routines.\\n22. Privacy and Security Analysis\\nThe integration of the Local REST API plugin requires serving HTTPS traffic on localhost (127.0.0.1) utilizing a self-signed TLS certificate. While this circumvents external cloud routing, exposing local ports theoretically allows malicious local scripts to access vault data. This risk is comprehensively mitigated by the API\'s enforcement of a 64-character Bearer [REDACTED] generated within the application. Vault data is never transmitted externally; the flow of information is strictly initiated by the user\'s verified, local AI agent, ensuring absolute data sovereignty.\\n23. Large-Vault Suitability Analysis\\nScaling to a 15,000-note vault requires a philosophical shift from dynamic, query-time data generation to static, deterministic data structures. Workflows reliant on inline properties, deep block-referencing queries, and continuous regex searches must be abandoned in favor of native YAML frontmatter and strict linting rules. Structuring data deterministically allows Obsidian\'s highly optimized core metadata cache to manage retrieval and relationship mapping without the massive hardware overhead required by third-party database abstractions.\\n24. Recommended Ideal Plugin Stack\\nThe finalized architecture is tiered to prioritize stability, ensuring that baseline operations remain unimpeded by experimental features.\\n| Tier | Plugin | Function |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| Essential (Always Enabled) | Local REST API | Exposes MCP endpoints for Claude/Codex integration. |\\n| Essential (Always Enabled) | Canvas Image LOD | Prevents WebGL/Chromium OOM crashes on visual boards. |\\n| Essential (Always Enabled) | Meta Bind | Drives operational and business workflow states. |\\n| High-Value (Enabled as Needed) | Obsidian Linter | Run manually or debounced; disable \\"Lint on Save\\". |\\n| High-Value (Enabled as Needed) | Advanced Canvas | Enhances mapping of LSAT logic and curricula. |\\n| Native Core Alternatives | Bases | Replaces Dataview for structured data retrieval. |\\n| Native Core Alternatives | Native Search | Replaces Omnisearch to conserve memory footprint. |\\n25. Recommended Installation Order\\nTo preserve metadata cache integrity and prevent simultaneous background indexing storms, deployment must follow a sequential protocol:\\n * Initialize the vault in Restricted Mode to baseline performance telemetry.\\n * Enable Core Plugins, specifically targeting Properties and Bases.\\n * Install Obsidian Linter, configure target rulesets, and execute a singular manual pass over the vault to normalize all frontmatter. Ensure continuous background hooks are disabled.\\n * Install Meta Bind to establish operational dashboards.\\n * Install Canvas Image LOD, followed subsequently by Advanced Canvas.\\n * Install the Local REST API, configure the self-signed certificate, extract the security Bearer [REDACTED], and register the MCP server with the external AI client.\\n26. Safe Testing Plan\\nValidating the AI-agent integration requires a controlled, observable workflow:\\n * Real Workflow: Command Claude Desktop to summarize a specific student\'s tutoring record and append a new action item to their file frontmatter.\\n * Testing Period: Five consecutive days of standard operational use.\\n * Measurable Success Criteria: The AI accurately retrieves the file, interprets the frontmatter, and utilizes the create_or_update_note tool without generating hallucinatory syntax.\\n * Expected Performance Effects: Spikes in CPU activity should only occur concurrently with the AI\'s HTTP requests.\\n * Measurement Protocol: Monitor macOS Activity Monitor specifically targeting the \\"Obsidian Helper (Renderer)\\" process, ensuring it remains below 500 MB during transactions.\\n * Reversal Procedure: Disable the Local REST API plugin and revoke trust for the self-signed certificate within the macOS Keychain.\\n * Final Disposition: Retain the configuration only if it repeatedly demonstrates a reduction in manual data manipulation.\\n27. Thirty-Day Evaluation Plan\\nLong-term stability requires staged evaluation across distinct functional domains.\\n| Phase | Focus Area | Success Metrics |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| Days 1-7 | Core Stability | Monitor startup times, ensuring initialization concludes in under five seconds. |\\n| Days 8-14 | Visual Rendering | Stress-test Canvas rendering with extensive LSAT logic trees; verify zooming does not drop frames below 30fps. |\\n| Days 15-21 | Data Normalization | Execute batch linting; ensure YAML frontmatter is flawlessly normalized without corrupting file contents. |\\n| Days 22-30 | Operational Integration | Integrate full business operations via Meta Bind; assess UI responsiveness during input toggling. |\\n28. Evidence Limitations\\nEmpirical performance data regarding Datacore remains highly limited, as the plugin has not yet achieved stable release. Maintainer claims of significant performance increases cannot be robustly verified against a 15,000-vault test under strict 8 GB RAM constraints. Furthermore, Electron memory volatility is highly dependent on exact Chromium rendering paths; memory consumption behavior may fluctuate unpredictably with minor Obsidian installer updates.\\n29. Unresolved Questions\\nThe future trajectory of the ecosystem hinges on whether the Obsidian core team will integrate native web worker multithreading APIs for community developers. If implemented, resource-intensive plugins such as Dataview or Omnisearch could regain viability on hardware-constrained systems. Additionally, as the Model Context Protocol specification continues to evolve rapidly, its long-term impact on the stability and feature set of the Local REST API integration remains an open variable requiring continuous monitoring.\\n\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

That means I can control multiple terminals from a single space, and that looks something like this. In fact, it\'s Herder acts as a server. So, even if I exit out of that window, it\'s still running in the background, and I can very easily bring it back up. I think of context as expertise and I think of connections as situational. So let me break that down so it you know actually makes more sense. So expertise context is the things that you actually need all the time. So you know who are you?\\n\\n**5:12** · What are your goals? What does your business do?\\n\\n**5:17** · That\'s expertise context. The way I like to think about this is an analogy of a principal and a teacher. Pretend they\'re trying to make a seating chart. Your policies, your pre-loaded information that needs to be in every single run. Sort of like the system prompt. Whereas the situational context is things that you need just in time. Then I use it to redesign the AI Automation Society website live and improve the first draft with a focused feedback pass.  \\n  \\nSponsorship Inquiries:  \\n📧 nate@smoothmedia.co  \\n  \\nConnect with me:  \\nhttps://www.linkedin.com/in/nateherkelman/  \\nhttps://x.com/nateherk  \\nhttps://www.instagram.com/nateherk/  \\n  \\nTIMESTAMPS  \\n0:00 Introducing Scrollcraft  \\n2:26 Get the Free Skill  \\n3:25 Live Website Redesign  \\n4:39 Generating Website Assets  \\n5:27 Defining the Scroll Journey  \\n8:15 Building & Verification  \\n9:11 Reviewing the First Draft  \\n12:16 Giving Claude Feedback  \\n14:11 Reviewing the Final Version  \\n16:22 Download Scrollcraft\\n\\n## Transcript\\n\\n### Introducing Scrollcraft\\n\\n**0:00** · I think I built the ultimate claw design skill for websites and I\'m going to give it to you guys for completely free. This thing helps you figure out what type of website you\'re looking for, generates you all the assets or takes the assets you already have and then creates an interactive scrolling style of website.\\n\\n**0:13** · So, it\'s not just your generic AI slop.\\n\\n**0:15** · You can see in this one we\'re literally going through a journey because this is like a travel agency that\'s trying to get clients to book in with them. Here\'s my original website that I had built with Claude. It\'s it\'s okay, right? It\'s nothing crazy. And what I did is I said, \\"Hey, Claude, look at my website. use this skill and transform it. And this is what it created. It kept all the same copy. It kept all the same branding and the same feel, but it turned it into something that\'s way more interactive and it almost like hooks you in to make you want to keep scrolling through the rest of the website to actually see what\'s to come.\\n\\n**0:42** · It has a way more premium feel than the way that you typically get your websites if you\'re building with Claude. So, I call this skill scroll craft because the whole idea is that you\'re actually having this hook in the viewer by making the scroll correlate to something that\'s going on on the actual page. So, on top of the idea that Claude should always be looking for opportunities to make the scroll feel interactive, there\'s also design elements in the skill as well.\\n\\n**1:04** · Things like spacing, typography, feel, taste, all that kind of stuff. And what you\'ll notice about the skill is it\'s not just one specific template, right?\\n\\n**1:12** · Every single one of these websites that I\'ve shown you guys so far comes out a little bit different. These are method and architecture sources about the OS itself, not business or LSAT knowledge. Siblings of [[obsidian-command-center]] and [[second-brain-levels]].\\r\\n\\r\\n- **\\"You\'re Using Claude Code Wrong (Add Codex)\\"** — Chase AI, YouTube `VdxUKiF8CWI`, 2026-05-07. The *why + the tandem workflow*.\\r\\n- **\\"How to Use Your Claude Code Projects in Codex in 5 Mins\\"** — Nate Herk, YouTube `kB9iMD0EjT8`, 2026-05-18. The *how of file structure* — one project, both agents.\\r\\n- **\\"This Repo Just Solved The #1 Claude + Codex Headache\\"** — Chase AI, YouTube `neK8ydl0Vlk`, 2026-07-16. The *execution and observability layer* using Herdr.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The pitch: it\'s \\"and,\\" not \\"or\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nDon\'t pick Claude Code *or* Codex. Run both on the same project and let each check the other. The Venn diagram is ~99% overlap, so learning one gets you the other for free. The argument underneath is **tool agnosticism**: you owe these companies zero loyalty, models are converging, and pitting them against each other gets better output than staying tribal. Practical upside beyond quality: if one tool is down or stuck, you switch in seconds without losing context.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The tandem workflow (Chase)\\r\\n\\r\\nRun Codex in its desktop app (openai.com/codex) with a terminal open inside it, then run `claude` in that terminal, so both operate in the same directory. Then bounce work between them:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Plan review.** Codex plans → copy the plan into Claude (\\"what\'s missing, any blind spots?\\") → paste Claude\'s critique back → Codex revises. Two expert second-opinions before any code. Works in reverse too.\\r\\n- **Adversarial code review.** One builds, the other reads the same files and hunts bugs. In the demo Claude found ~20 real bugs on Codex\'s first pass. Telling the reviewer the *other* model wrote it may make it usefully more adversarial. There is a Codex plugin skill for Claude literally called \\"adversarial review.\\"\\r\\n- **Cost logic.** Uses more tokens up front, but catching bugs and weak plans early saves tokens in aggregate.\\r\\n- Desktop-app niceties Chase leans on: in-app browser + sidebar dev server, click-to-annotate/screenshot front-end reviews, diff viewer, \\"pets\\" background-activity indicator. Context note: GPT-5.5 Pro ~258k window vs Claude\'s ~1M; new chat = the `/clear` equivalent.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The file-structure map (Nate) — one project, both agents\\r\\n\\r\\nBoth agents read the **same shared knowledge** (docs, references, scripts, context files). Only the tool-specific wrappers differ:\\r\\n\\r\\n| Concern | Claude Code | Codex |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Instructions file | `CLAUDE.md` | `AGENTS.md` |\\r\\n| Config folder | `.claude/` | `.codex/` |\\r\\n| Skills folder | `.claude/skills/` | `.agents/skills/` |\\r\\n| Skill files | markdown + YAML front matter | **identical** markdown |\\r\\n| Agent files | markdown | **TOML** (same job, different format) |\\r\\n| Both support | global (`~/.claude`, `~/.codex`) + project-level scope | same |\\r\\n\\r\\n**3-layer mental model:** (1) shared knowledge any agent reads unchanged, (2) skills + agents — same content, just placed in each tool\'s folder, (3) tool-specific config that genuinely differs. Maintenance rule: a major change to `CLAUDE.md` gets mirrored to `AGENTS.md` (and skills/agents to their twin). Codex sub-agents don\'t auto-invoke — you call them explicitly.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Convert-any-project prompt (Nate\'s):**\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nI built this project using Claude Code, but I need you, Codex, to be able\\r\\nto use it too. Create an AGENTS.md that uses the CLAUDE.md as inspiration,\\r\\ncreate a .codex config, put all skills in .agents, put all agents in .codex\\r\\n(as TOML). Research both the Codex and Claude Code documentation and make\\r\\nsure everything important converts over.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n**Session handoff** (Nate\'s own skill) is the bridge: summarize decisions / active files / next steps, paste into the other tool, keep going. When one gets stuck, hand off and the other often unsticks it in seconds.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Herdr makes the tandem workflow visible and persistent\\r\\n\\r\\nHerdr is the terminal and session layer around Claude Code and Codex. It does not replace either agent or the shared-file architecture. It gives the workflow an operational surface:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Workspaces** separate projects.\\r\\n- **Tabs** separate activities within a project.\\r\\n- **Panes** show Claude Code, Codex, servers, tests, and helper agents side by side.\\r\\n- **Agent status** rolls up which pane is working, idle, or blocked.\\r\\n- **Persistence** keeps pane processes running after the Herdr client detaches or its terminal window closes. Reopen `herdr` to reconnect. Use `herdr server stop` only when the processes should actually stop.\\r\\n- **Agent control** lets an agent with the Herdr skill inspect neighboring panes, split panes, run commands, read output, and wait for another agent.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Installed setup on Joshua\'s Windows machine\\r\\n\\r\\nInstalled and verified on 2026-09-22:\\r\\n\\r\\n| Component | Installed state |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| Herdr | Stable `0.9.1`; installed under `C:\\\\Users\\\\Joshu\\\\.herdr\\\\packages\\\\standalone\\\\releases\\\\0.9.1-x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`; user PATH updated |\\r\\n| Claude Code | `2.1.280`; installed in the shared Node tools directory so Herdr panes can resolve it |\\r\\n| Codex CLI | `0.156.1`; installed in the shared Node tools directory so Herdr panes can resolve it |\\r\\n| Herdr integrations | Claude `v10` and Codex `v8`, both current |\\r\\n| Herdr agent skill | Installed globally for Claude Code and Codex |\\r\\n\\r\\nOpen a new PowerShell window so it receives the updated user PATH. Then:\\r\\n\\r\\n```powershell\\r\\ncd C:\\\\Users\\\\Joshu\\\\dev\\\\ai-os\\r\\nherdr\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nInside Herdr, use the first pane for Claude Code:\\r\\n\\r\\n```powershell\\r\\nclaude\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nSplit right with the mouse menu or press `Ctrl+B`, release, then press `V`. In the new pane:\\r\\n\\r\\n```powershell\\r\\ncodex\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nRecommended layout:\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pane | Purpose |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| Left | Claude Code as the primary operator or planner |\\r\\n| Right | Codex as reviewer, critic, or independent implementer |\\r\\n| Optional bottom pane | Dev server, tests, or logs |\\r\\n\\r\\nHerdr improves visibility but does not change the shared-worktree guardrail: Claude and Codex may read the same files, but they must not edit the same file at the same time. Assign explicit file ownership or use a worktree when both are implementing.\\r\\n\\r\\nFirst-run checks:\\r\\n\\r\\n```powershell\\r\\nherdr integration status\\r\\nherdr agent list\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nIf an agent is detected incorrectly, run `herdr agent explain <target> --json`. The Windows config file is `%APPDATA%\\\\herdr\\\\config.toml`; no custom config is required for the initial setup.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Where ai-os stands (gap-check, 2026-09-22)\\r\\n\\r\\nai-os **already runs this exact pattern** — it was built dual-runtime from the reform (see `wiki/log.md` 2026-07-17, and [[../decisions/log.md]]). Point-by-point:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **`CLAUDE.md` + `AGENTS.md` hand-owned twins** — present, mirrored, near-identical byte size. The only diffs are the *intentional* Claude↔Codex swaps (twin pointer, folder paths, the dual-runtime section written from each runtime\'s view). Voice section is in full parity. **No drift.**\\r\\n- **`.claude/` and `.codex/` config folders** — both present (`.codex/hooks.json` mirrors the SessionStart bootstrap).\\r\\n- **Skills as identical markdown, activated by copy** — `.claude/skills/` (22) and `.agents/skills/` (12); Codex runs the same business set minus the wiki/Obsidian/graphify/youtube extras, which is deliberate, not a gap.\\r\\n- **`session-handoff` skill** — Nate\'s skill, already installed and active in both runtimes.\\r\\n- **Agents as TOML for Codex** — catalog holds `agents/codex/web-researcher.toml`. Note: that agent is authored to activate **globally** (its module path is `~/.codex/agents/web-search-modules/`), so it is not project-activated into `~/ai-os/.codex/agents/`, and that is by design, not missing wiring.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Conclusion:** the first two clips validated the shared-file architecture. The third exposed a real execution gap: there was no persistent, visible session layer for running both agents together. Herdr now fills that gap on this Windows machine. The remaining human step is Claude authentication on first launch.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What\'s new vs already-known\\r\\n\\r\\n- **New and implemented:** Herdr as the visible, persistent execution layer for Claude Code and Codex; direct integrations and the Herdr agent skill are installed for both.\\r\\n- **Worth adopting as a habit:** the plan↔critique and adversarial-review ping-pong between the two models on genuinely hard or high-stakes work; reach for the Codex desktop app\'s live browser + annotation when doing front-end review.\\r\\n- **Already true in ai-os:** the whole file-structure map, twin instruction files, copy-based skill activation, session-handoff bridge, tool-agnostic stance.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n## Claudex skills installed globally (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nCurrent upstream: https://github.com/chaseai-yt/claudex-loop, installed from commit `8cf5e2c1771c5151d90c12642391d0ba8fa71b0e`. Clone: `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os-projects/claudex-loop`, outside the vault. All four current skills (`claudex-route`, `claudex-loop`, `codex-review`, `codex-build`) and both local compatibility aliases (`grill-me-codex`, `grill-with-docs-codex`) are complete copies in both catalogs and project activation directories, plus global `.agents/skills`, `.codex/skills`, and `.claude/skills`. No linking into the application directory.\\r\\n\\r\\nPrefer `claudex-route` for one scoped handoff, and `claudex-loop` for repeated independent review and implementation. The host-aware loop sends Codex work to Claude for review and Claude work to Codex. Both grill names now delegate to this current loop; the docs variant preserves context, glossary, and architecture decisions. Local alias sources live in `system/skill-overrides/claudex`. The updater copies these instead of upstream legacy instructions. Shared references and Python runner ship with the complete current package. Existing conversations may need a new session to refresh discovery.\\r\\n\\r\\nBoth CLIs authenticated on 2026-09-30: Codex 0.156.1 and Claude Code 2.1.280. Metadata validation, 23 package tests, and both role-routing checks pass. These checks do not establish a completed live model review; the earlier stalled review remains a failed run.\\r\\n\\r\\nAutomatic updater: `system/scripts/sync-claudex-skills.py`. Codex app automation `update-claudex-skills` runs daily at 07:00 America/Toronto while the local automation host is available. It fast-forwards the clean clone and updates only seven declared skill names, preserving older copies under `~/.agents/claudex-backups` and refusing to overwrite locally edited trees. State is `~/.agents/claudex-sync-state.json`. Run without `--install` for routine updates; that flag explicitly adopts existing versions and is only for supervised initial installation. No deletions, commits, pushes, CLI upgrades or review/build launches occur during sync. Skills INDEX is regenerated after changes.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Ask Codex to grill by Claude\\r\\n\\r\\n`grill-by-claude` is a locally maintained entry point installed in both catalogs and all five activation roots. Say \\"grill by Claude\\" in Codex: Codex interviews and prepares the plan, calls the authenticated Claude Code CLI for independent review, evaluates findings, and revises within the shared loop limits. Review alone does not authorize implementation. Verified runner roles select Codex planner and Claude reviewer; this installation check did not perform a live model review. The updater maintains this alias alongside the other two local compatibility names.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Every Claudex skill has the inverse Codex direction (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nSupersedes the shared-entry-point installation details above. All six package skills now have runtime-specific entry points. Claude installations coordinate toward Codex; Codex installations coordinate toward Claude. codex-review retains its familiar name but asks Claude to review in Codex. codex-build delegates to Claude in Codex, using build --host codex --builder claude and fresh inspect --host codex --builder claude. The Claude copy retains Codex builder and Claude inspector. The loop defaults to the host builder with other-provider inspection, and route defaults to the other provider for cross-provider handoffs while honoring explicit user choices.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe updater renders separate entry points and hashes for each runtime, preserving complete upstream supporting files. Codex review/build adaptations are locally maintained in system/skill-overrides/claudex/codex; future upstream support updates do not overwrite these intentional directions. Seven roots contain matching copies for their runtime. Verified metadata, both inverse role maps, idempotent repeat sync, and preservation of edited targets. These checks did not launch a live provider review/build.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Codex entry points renamed (2026-09-30)\\n\\r\\nSupersedes retained-name descriptions above. Codex now exposes claude-review, claude-build, grill-me-claude, and grill-with-docs-claude. Claude retains the corresponding Codex names. claudex-loop and claudex-route remain unchanged in both runtimes. The sync maps source names to installed names, updates frontmatter, and moves previously managed old Codex copies into inactive backups after verifying their replacements. Edited old copies are preserved as conflicts. The grill-by-claude shortcut remains available.\\n\\n### Stateful UI work orders require transition evidence (2026-10-01)\\n\\nAfter the LSAT U study-tools review, Joshua requested durable prevention guidance for Claude. Future delegated stateful UI work orders must specify state ownership and lifetime, then require actor-switch isolation, multiple edits during failed persistence, selected-answer/cross-out consistency, reducer-committed persistence under rapid interactions, and touch/keyboard accessibility checks where applicable. Include these acceptance checks in the actual work order; do not rely on remembered guidance alone. Inspect the final mixed snapshot after fixes and distinguish tested behavior from blocked browser checks.\\n\\nThe detailed failure cases and corrective instructions are indexed in Claude\'s native ai-os memory as `lsatu-study-tool-transition-checks.md`. Reviewers must trace existing behavior before calling it missing: the initial LR-find omission claim was false. Memory is guidance; regression tests and independent inspection provide enforcement. Keep Claude and Codex file ownership separate and preserve all existing student records.\\n\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text':
